(a) starting from the formulation with matter sites and truncated SU(2) gauge links, (b) we split the latter in pairs of rishon modes
\hat{\zeta}
defined in
Eq.

1.3.27
and constrain their dynamics with the SU(2) link symmetry in
Eq.

1.3.36
.
We then (c) merge them with matter fields into dressed SU(2) gauge-singlets with a bosonic statistics. As a consequence, within the dressed-site formalism, the hopping always involves an even number of fermions, i.e. the matter field plus a rishon mode (d).
Figure inspired by
[
437
,
440
]
.

As discussed in
Section

1.2.1
, to make LGT Hamiltonians in
Eqs.

1.3.7
and
1.3.8
suitable for TN methods and quantum hardware, a finite-dimensional gauge-link Hilbert space is typically required.
Here, we propose an effective truncation for the SU(2) gauge fields that can be applied to lattices of any spatial dimension and scalable to arbitrarily large truncations.
In the limit of an infinitely large spin irreducible representation of the SU(2) gauge group, it recovers all the properties of the original SU(2) Yang-Mills LGT.
Remarkably, the use of this approach in the smallest non-trivial truncation of SU(2), labeled as
hardcore-gluon
approximation, has been used to achieve non-trivial results as the ones discussed in
Chapter

3

[
437
]
and
Section

4.4

[
578
,
438
]
.
Of course, to accurately represent the full theory, for weak-
g
, larger gauge representations are required: this increases the computational challenges but it is still potentially accessible via TNs.
Anyway, such a description of the gauge group is the first building block for building logical dressed-sites discussed in
Section

1.2
, where Gauss Law is already satisfied (see
Fig.

1.2
).

To truncate the continuous SU(2) gauge group, we express it in terms of the irreducible representation (irrep) basis
[
595
,
598
]
.
As SU(2) admits a
quasi-real
-representation, where the fundamental and the anti-fundamental representations coincide,
\forall{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}{\in}\Lambda
, the gauge Hilbert space of the
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
link can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauge}}

\displaystyle=\quantity{\ket{\spin,\mL,\mR}},

where

\displaystyle-j{\leq}m_{L(R)}{\leq}j

(1.3.9)

is the corresponding third spin component associated with the left (right) side of the SU(2) link-irrep
j
.
Then, the parallel transporter
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
is given in terms of Clebsh-Gordan coefficients:

\bra{\spin^{\prime}\mL^{\prime}\mR^{\prime}}\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}\ket{\spin\mL\mR}=\sqrt{\frac{2j+1}{2j^{\prime}+1}}\overline{C^{jm_{\mathrm{L}}}_{\hskip-0.7pt{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-0.7pt,\alpha;j^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}}}C^{j^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}}_{jm_{\mathrm{R}};\hskip-0.7pt{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-0.7pt,\beta}.

(1.3.10)

Correspondingly, left and right generators are defined as:

\displaystyle\langle j^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}|\hat{L}^{a}|jm_{\mathrm{L}}m_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}S^{(j)a}_{m_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime},m_{\mathrm{L}}}\delta_{m_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime},m_{\mathrm{R}}},

(1.3.11a)

\displaystyle\langle j^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}m_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}|\hat{R}^{a}|jm_{\mathrm{L}}m_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{m_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime},m_{\mathrm{L}}}S^{(j)a}_{m_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime},m_{\mathrm{R}}}\,.

(1.3.11b)

Then, the chromo-electric energy operator, i.e. the quadratic Casimir in
Eq.

1.3.2
reads
[
565
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{E}^{2}\ket{\spin\mL\mR}

\displaystyle=j(j+1)\ket{\spin\mL\mR}.

(1.3.12)

Then, the truncation of the gauge fields is applied by imposing a local energy cutoff
\Theta
on the
\hat{E}^{2}
spectrum, keeping the irreps
j\leq j_{\max}
such that
C_{2}(j_{\max})\leq\Theta
.
The truncation keeps the electric field operator
\hat{E}
hermitian and protects the SU(2) gauge algebra rules of
Eqs.

1.3.1
and
1.3.3
.
Namely, truncated
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
fields satisfy the following left and right gauge transformations:

\displaystyle\quantity[\eleL^{a}_{\jmax},\NApara]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\gamma}\frac{\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\gamma}}{2}\hat{U}_{\gamma\beta}

\displaystyle\quantity[\eleR^{a}_{\jmax},\Apara_{\alpha\beta}]

\displaystyle=+\sum_{\gamma}\hat{U}_{\alpha\gamma}\frac{\sigma^{a}_{\gamma\beta}}{2},

(1.3.13)

where
\hat{L}^{a}_{j_{\max}}
and
\hat{R}^{a}_{j_{\max}}
are (truncated) generators of the left- and right-handed groups of SU(2) transformations.
They can be expressed as the block-diagonal direct sum of spin matrices
S^{a}_{j}
in consecutive
j
-representations from the smallest (
j=0
) to the largest one (
j=j_{\max}
):

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{a}_{j_{\max}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{j_{\max}}\quantity(S^{a}_{j}{\otimes} \mathbb{1}_{j})=\rm{diag}(S^{a}_{0}{\otimes}\mathbb{1}_{0},\dots,S^{a}_{j_{\max}}{\otimes}\mathbb{1}_{j_{\max}})

(1.3.14)

\displaystyle\hat{R}^{a}_{j_{\max}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{j_{\max}}\quantity(\mathbb{1}_{j}{\otimes}S^{a}_{j})=\rm{diag}(\mathbb{1}_{0}{\otimes}S^{a}_{0},\dots,\mathbb{1}_{j_{\max}}{\otimes}S^{a}_{j_{\max}}).

Unfortunately, the truncation makes
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
unitary only as long as it acts on spin shells with
j<j_{\max}
(it loses norm on the largest spin shell).
Correspondingly, Wilson loops stay unitary as long as the outer spin shell
j=j_{\max}
is nowhere populated.
Namely, it implies that,
\forall j<j_{\max}

\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\gamma}}\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}{\ket{0{;}0{,}0}}{\otimes}{\hat{U}_{\beta\gamma}}{\ket{j{;}\mL{,}\mR}}{\otimes}{\hat{U}_{\gamma\delta}}{\ket{0{;}0{,}0}}

(1.3.15)

displays the same norm of
\hat{U}_{\alpha\delta}\ket{000}

\forall\alpha,\delta
, that is
1/\sqrt{2}
.
Ultimately, we require the parallel transporter to display
spatial reflection symmetry
.
Namely:

\hat{U}^{\dagger}_{\alpha\beta}=-\hat{\mathcal{F}}\hat{U}_{\beta\alpha}\hat{\mathcal{F}},

(1.3.16)

where
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
is the
swap operator
on a gauge link:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\sum_{j,m_{\mathrm{L}},m_{\mathrm{R}}}{\ket{\spin;\mL,\mR}}{\bra{\spin;\mR,\mL}}=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\dagger}\,.

(1.3.17)

Whatever the gauge field truncation, in hopping terms, the action of
\hat{U}
has to match the fundamental irrep of the matter field, whose Hilbert space in the Fock space, can be written as:

\mathcal{H}_{\text{site}}=\quantity{
\ket{0}=\ket{\Omega},\;
\ket{\rla}=\psi^{\dagger}_{\rla}\ket{\Omega},\;
\ket{\gla}=\psi^{\dagger}_{\gla}\ket{\Omega},\;
\ket{2}=\psi^{\dagger}_{\rla}
\psi^{\dagger}_{\gla}\ket{\Omega}},

(1.3.18)

whose action in the irrep basis
\ket{\spin,m}
corresponds to the following SU(2) charges:

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{\text{site}}=\quantity{\ket{0,0},\ket{\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}},\ket{\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$};-\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}},\ket{0,0}}.\end{split}

(1.3.19)

1.3.3
Rishon decomposition of SU(2) gauge fields

With all these premises, the resulting truncated SU(2) Yang-Mills Hamiltonian of
Eq.

1.3.7
-(
1.3.8
) will act on the Hilbert space resulting from the tensor product of all the single matter Hilbert spaces and the corresponding ones of each truncated gauge field:

\mathcal{H}=\bigotimes_{\mathbf{n}}\mathcal{H}_{\text{matt}}\bigotimes_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}\mathcal{H}_{\rm{tr.link}}\,.

(1.3.20)

Notice that matter and gauge fields display different statistics.
In these terms, we expect
\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha\beta}
to be
mutually bosonic
, as it commutes with all the matter-fields operators:

\quantity[\Apara_{\genlink}^{\alpha\beta},\hpsi^{(\dagger)}_{\vecsite,\alpha}]=0\qquad\forall\mathbf{n},\forall{\bf\it\mu},\forall\alpha,\beta

(1.3.21)

and
purely local
, as its link-algebra commutes with the one of any other link:

\displaystyle\quantity[\NApara_{\genlink},\Apara_{\vecsite^{\prime},\vecsite^{\prime}+\latvec^{\prime}}^{\gamma\delta}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{n}^{\prime},{\bf\it\mu}\neq{\bf\it\mu}^{\prime},\forall\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\,.

(1.3.22)

As anticipated in
Section

1.2
, in the electric basis, the parallel transporter
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
admits a rishon-decomposition to arbitrary truncation of the maximum allowed spin shell
j_{\max}
.
Starting from a given spin shell
j
, we have to separately account for the action when both rishons are increased to shell
j+\frac{1}{2}
, and both are decreased to shell
j-\frac{1}{2}
.
We can then decompose
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
as follows:

\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=\hat{\zeta}_{A,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{B,{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger}+\hat{\zeta}_{B,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{A,{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger},

(1.3.23)

where the two
\hat{\zeta}
-rishon species, A and B, act respectively as raising and lowering the spin shell of the SU(2) gauge irrep. Interestingly, they are related to each other as:

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{A}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{\zeta}_{B}^{\beta\dagger}

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{A}^{\alpha\dagger}=i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{\zeta}_{B}^{\beta}\,.

(1.3.24)

We can then rewrite
Eq.

1.3.23
in terms of one species, e.g. B. Renaming
\hat{\zeta}_{B}^{\alpha}=\hat{\zeta}^{\alpha}
, we obtain:

\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha\gamma}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\gamma\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger}+i\sigma^{y}_{\beta\gamma}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\gamma}\,,

(1.3.25)

or equivalently

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}\dagger}+\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

(1.3.26)

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}\dagger}-\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=-\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}\dagger}+\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}=-\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}\dagger}-\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\,.

For a chosen truncation
j_{\max}
of the SU(2) irreducible representation,
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons are defined as:

\hat{\zeta}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r})}=\quantity[\sum_{\spin=0}^{\jmax-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{m=-\spin}^{\spin}\chi(\spin,m,\gla(\rla)) \ket{\spin,m}\bra{\spin+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$},m{\cmp}\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}}]_{F}\,,

(1.3.27)

where the function
\chi(j,m,\alpha)
reads

\chi\quantity(\spin,m,\gla(\rla))=\sqrt{\frac{j\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{-0.81668pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-1.66666pt\hbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\SavedStyle-$}\hskip-1.66666pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{-2.33333pt}{\hbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\SavedStyle+$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 3.8889pt}$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}m+1}{\sqrt{(2j+1)(2j+2)}}}\,.

(1.3.28)

It is possible to show that this construction is indeed compatible with the explicit form of the parallel transport reported in
Eq.

1.3.10
.

SU(2) Rishon Parity

Being fermions,
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons anti-commute at different orbitals and with matter fields:

\displaystyle\quantity{\rishon_{\genlink}^{\alpha},\rishon_{\siteplus,-\latvec}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\quantity{\rishon_{\genlink}^{\alpha},\hpsi_{\vecsite,\beta}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall\alpha,\beta\,.

(1.3.29)

To satisfy
Eq.

1.3.29
, we need to characterize them as fermion operators properly.
As discussed in
Section

1.2.4
, we can then introduce the notion of a parity for the SU(2) rishon modes.
Similarly to
Sections

1.2.4
and
1.2.4
, we define the SU(2) rishon parity operator
\hat{P}_{\zeta}
with an even (
+1
) parity sector on
integer
irreps and odd (
-1
) sector on
semi-integer
ones:

\hat{P}_{\zeta}=\rm{diag}(+1|-1,-1|+1,+1,+1|\dots)\,.

(1.3.30)

The action of the single rishon inverts the local parity:
\anticommutator*{\parity}{\rishon^\alpha}=0
.
Correspondingly, the parallel transporter
\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha\beta}
reads:

\begin{split}\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha\beta}=&i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha\gamma}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\gamma\dagger}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger}+i\sigma^{y}_{\beta\gamma}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\gamma}\\
=&+i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha\gamma}\quantity(\rishon_{\genlink}^{\gamma\dagger}\cdot\parity_{\zeta, \genlink})\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger}+i\sigma^{y}_{\beta\gamma}\quantity(\rishon_{\genlink}^{\alpha}\cdot\parity_{\zeta, \genlink})\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\gamma}\,.\end{split}

(1.3.31)

Notice that the right-hand side of
Eq.

1.3.31
preserves the SU(2) link parity, as desired.

SU(2) Rishon Algebra

Instead of relying on two separate set of SU(2) generators,
\hat{L}^{a}
and
\hat{R}^{a}
,
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons have a unique gauge transformation algebra.
The generators of SU(2) gauge rotations upon the
\hat{\zeta}
-rishon space read:

\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{j_{\max}}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{j_{\max}}S^{a}_{j}=\rm{diag}(S^{a}_{0},S^{a}_{1},\dots S^{a}_{j_{\max}})\,.

(1.3.32)

By construction,
\hat{\zeta}
operators are SU(2) covariant, as they transform as follows:

\displaystyle\quantity[\rishon^{\alpha},\tgenerator^{a}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta}\hat{\sigma}^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\hat{\zeta}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\quantity[\tgenerator^{a},\rishon_{\alpha}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta}\hat{\zeta}^{\beta\dagger}\hat{\sigma}^{a}_{\beta\alpha}\,.

(1.3.33)

Moreover,
\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}
is genuinely
local
: for
\forall\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
,
\forall{\bf\it\mu}\neq{\bf\it\mu}^{\prime}
, and
\forall\alpha\in\quantity{\rla,\gla}
:

\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}},\hat{\zeta}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},+{\bf\it\mu}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\big]=\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}},\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\alpha}\big]=0\,.

(1.3.34)

We easily recover the left and right generators of the gauge field at link
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
as:

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{a}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}{\otimes}\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}

\displaystyle\hat{R}^{a}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}{\otimes}\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}\,.

(1.3.35)

Since in the SU(2) group the fundamental and the anti-fundamental representations coincide, the rishon formalism is meaningful as long as the quadratic Casimir operator of the two sides of the link coincide as in
Eq.

1.1.55
:

\absolutevalue{\eleL^{a}_{\vecsite, +\latvec}}^{2}=\absolutevalue{\eleR^{a}_{\siteplus, -\latvec}}^{2}\,.

(1.3.36)

Thanks to
Eq.

1.3.36
, the two rishons of the link are in the same SU(2) irrep, and the parallel transport in
Eq.

1.3.23
coincides with the one in
Eq.

1.3.10
.
Correspondingly, the Casimir operator of the
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
link in
Eq.

1.1.55
can be expressed as:

\begin{split}&\hat{E}^{2}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\abs{\eleL^{a}_{\vecsite,+\latvec}}^{2} +\abs{\eleR^{a}_{\siteplus,-\latvec}}^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\abs{\tgenerator^{a}_{\vecsite,+\latvec}}^{2} +\abs{\tgenerator^{a}_{\siteplus,-\latvec}}^{2}]\,,\end{split}

(1.3.37)

which looks explicitly symmetric under link reversal.

1.3.4
Constructing dressed site operators

We have then all the ingredients to build a
dressed-site
compact representation. In the case of a 2D lattice, one possible pictorial description of
dressed-site
states reads:

\displaystyle\ket{\begin{array}{ccc}
&\rishon_{\vecsite,+\latvec[y]}&\\
\rishon_{\vecsite,-\latvec[x]}&\qty(\hpsi_{\vecsite,\rla}\hpsi_{\vecsite,\gla})&\rishon_{\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\\
&\rishon_{\vecsite,-\latvec[y]}&\\
\end{array}},

where

\displaystyle\ket{\begin{array}{ccc}
&5&\\
2&(0,1)&4\\
&3&\\
\end{array}}

is a possible internal ordering to be used as in
Eq.

1.2.6
when constructing composite operators out of matter fields and rishons inside the dressed site.

We can then rewrite the SU(2) Yang-Mills Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.3.7
-(
1.3.8
) via rishon modes.

Arrival operators

Let us start with the hopping Hamiltonian term. Discarding all the pre-factors, we have
2
2

2

Just for practical implementation in numerical simulations, the definition of the arrival operators here recalls the parallel transporter definition in terms of the two rishon species. Of course, an equivalent definition in terms of only one of the two species is available.
:

\begin{split}\hat{H}^{\text{hopping}}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}&=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha\beta}\hat{\psi}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},\beta}\\
&=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\quantity[\rishon_{A,\genlink}^{\alpha} \rishon_{B,\siteplus,-\latvec}^{\beta\dagger}
{+}\rishon_{B,\genlink}^{\alpha} \rishon_{A,\siteplus,-\latvec}^{\beta\dagger}]\hat{\psi}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},\beta}\\
&=\quantity[\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{A,\genlink}\hat{Q}_{B,\siteplus,-\latvec}+\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite}\hat{Q}_{A,\siteplus,-\latvec}],\end{split}

where we defined two species of
arrival
operators:

\displaystyle\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{A,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{A,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{B,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{B,{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}.

(1.3.44)

These operators’ practical construction must be consistent with the internal order in
Section

1.3.4
.

Matter number density operators

Similarly, number density operators are expressed as

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\psi_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}\bigotimes_{{\bf\it\mu}}\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\otimes\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\psi_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}\bigotimes_{{\bf\it\mu}}\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}

(1.3.45)

and give access to other observables like:

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{tot}}

\displaystyle=\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}+\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{pair}}

\displaystyle=\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{single}}

\displaystyle=\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{tot}}-\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{pair}}\,,

(1.3.46)

which respectively measure the total matter density and the corresponding occupancy of pairs or single particles.

Casimir operator

As we assumed via
Eq.

1.3.37
that each of the two
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons equally contributes to the link-electric energy density, we can equivalently define a dressed-site operator summing the Casimir contributions from its attached rishons:

\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\bf\it\mu}}(\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}})^{2}\,.

(1.3.47)

Magnetic Operators

Expressing
\hat{U}
as in
Eq.

1.3.23
, the plaquette term gives rise to 16 contributions:

\begin{split}\hat{U}_{\square}&=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\delta,\gamma}\quantity[\Apara_{\genlink_x}^{\alpha\beta}
{\Apara_{\siteplus_x,\siteplus_x+\latvec[y]}^{\beta\gamma}}
{\Apara_{\siteplus_x+\latvec[y],\siteplus_y}^{\gamma\delta}}
{\Apara_{\siteplus_y,\vecsite}^{\delta\alpha}}]\\
&=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\delta,\gamma}\quantity(\begin{array}{ccc}
\ulcorner&\rishon^{\delta\dagger}_{B,+\latvec[x]} \rishon_{A,-\latvec[x]}^{\gamma}& \urcorner\\
\rishon_{A,-\latvec[y]}^{\delta}& & \rishon^{\gamma\dagger}_{B,-\latvec[y]}\\
\rishon^{\alpha\dagger}_{B,+\latvec[y]}& &\rishon_{A,+\latvec[y]}^{\beta}\\
\llcorner& \rishon_{A,+\latvec[x]}^{\alpha} \rishon^{\beta\dagger}_{B,-\latvec[x]}&\lrcorner\\
\end{array})+\dots\\
&\underset{*}{=}-[\quantity(\begin{array}{ccc}
C^{AA}_{-\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]}&\rule{0.4cm}{0.5 pt}& C^{AA}_{-\latvec[x],-\latvec[y]}\\
|& &|\\
C^{AA}_{+\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]}&\rule{0.4cm}{0.5 pt}&C^{AA}_{+\latvec[y],-\latvec[x]}]\\
\end{array})+\dots,\end{split}

where, in
*
, we combined rishons in pairs to form
corner operators
like the following:

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{AA}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}^{\alpha\dagger}_{A,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}=\sum_{\alpha,\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}}i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha,\kappa}\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}^{\kappa\dagger}i\sigma^{y}_{\alpha,\kappa^{\prime}}\hat{\zeta}^{\kappa^{\prime}}_{B,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}

(1.3.48)

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{BB}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}^{\alpha\dagger}_{B,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}=\hat{C}^{AA}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{AB}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}^{\alpha\dagger}_{B,\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}=\quantity(\hat{C}^{BA}_{\vecsite,\latvec[2],\latvec[1]})^{\dagger}.

As for the previous dressed-site operators, the practical construction of corner operators has to be consistent with the internal ordering in
Section

1.3.4
.

SU(2) Link Symmetry

Within the dressed-site formalism, the condition in
Eq.

1.3.36
requiring the two rishon of the links to display the same Casimir operator (i.e.to be in the same spin shell
j
) is simply an Abelian
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
Link Symmetry.
It can be then easily encoded in ED and TN libraries employing symmetries
[
484
,
441
]
.

Projecting the operators on the SU(2) gauge invariant subspace

All the dressed-site operators previously derived must be projected in the SU(2) gauge-invariant basis
M
.
The latter is made by SU(2) singlets and can be determined as the kernel of the Gauss Law operators
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{\mathbf{n}}^{a}
:

\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot M=\quantity[\mattercasimir+\sum_{\latvec}\sum_{a}\tgenerator_{\vecsite, \latvec}^{a}\tgenerator_{\vecsite, \latvec}^{a}]\cdot M=0,

(1.3.49)

where
\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{a}_{{\bf\it\mu}}
are the
\hat{\zeta}
-rishon generators along the
{\bf\it\mu}^{\rm{th}}
direction and defined in
Eq.

1.3.32
, while
\hat{S}^{2}_{\rm{matt},\mathbf{n}}
is the matter color density introduced in
Eq.

1.3.6
.
In the minimal
j_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}
truncation of SU(2), the gauge-invariant Hilbert space of every dressed site of the full Hamiltonian has 30 gauge invariant states, whereas, restricting to the pure theory, the local Hilbert space is 9-dimensional.

The operative defermionized Hamiltonian

We are then ready to rewrite the SU(2) lattice Yang-Mills Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.3.7
making use of dressed-site operators in
Eqs.

1.3.44
and
1.3.48
.
Namely, we have:

\begin{split}{\hat{H}}{=}&{-}\frac{c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\quantity[\qty[i\qty[\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\hat{Q}_{B,\siteplus_x,-\latvec[x]}]{+}( -1)^{\site[x]+\site[y]}\qty[\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[y]}\hat{Q}_{B,\siteplus_y,-\latvec[y]}]{+}(A{\rightleftarrows}B)]+\hc]\\
&+m_{0}c^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}(-1)^{n_{x}+n_{y}}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n},\text{tot}}+\frac{c\hbar g^{2}}{4}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}\\
&-\frac{c\hbar}{2a_{0}g^{2}}\sum_{\square}\Tr\quantity[\qty[-\begin{matrix}
\hat{C}^{AA}_{\ulcorner} &\hat{C}^{AA}_{\urcorner}\\
\hat{C}^{AA}_{\llcorner} &\hat{C}^{AA}_{\lrcorner}
\end{matrix}
+\begin{matrix}
\hat{C}^{BB}_{\ulcorner} &\hat{C}^{AB}_{\urcorner}\\
\hat{C}^{AA}_{\llcorner} &\hat{C}^{AB}_{\lrcorner}
\end{matrix}+\dots]+\hc].\end{split}

(1.3.50)

Not surprisingly,
Eq.

1.3.50
is completely
bosonic
, as all the dressed-site operators are made out of pairs of fermions (matter field + rishon, pairs of matter fields, or rishon pairs).
Then, fermionic degrees of freedom are completely hidden inside each dressed site and there is no more need to face anti-commutation rules (see
Fig.

1.2
(d)).

1.3.5
Hardcore-gluon model: minimally truncated gauge-fields

As an example, we consider the
hardcore-gluon
approximation, corresponding to the smallest non-trivial representations of the gauge fields, obtained truncating the Casimir up to
j_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}
.
In analogy to cold quantum gases, the label
hardcore-gluon
aims to stress that the only accessible local configurations are those states reachable from the bare vacuum with at most one application of
\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}
.
Namely, we consider
(0{\otimes}0){\oplus}(\frac{1}{2}{\otimes}\frac{1}{2})
as the gauge field space, where
(j)
is the irreducible representation of SU(2)
[
531
,
532
,
534
,
599
,
600
]
.
Such a truncation is enough to guarantee the contribution of all the terms in the Hamiltonian
Eqs.

1.3.7
and
1.3.8
, and provides a good approximation of the untruncated low energy physics in the strong coupling limit
g\gg 1
, where the gauge field energy term dominates the Hamiltonian.
Remarkably, it has been succesfully adopted in
[
437
,
438
,
578
]
to extract interesting physics in and out of equilibrium (see
Chapters

3
and
4.4
respectively).

Within the
hardcore-gluon
approximation, the gauge-link Hilbert space reduces to the states
\ket{\spin, m}
with
j_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}
.
In analogy to the fundamental representation of SU(2), we can parametrize the
m=\pm\frac{1}{2}
values with the colors
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}
respectively:

\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauge}}=\quantity{\ket{0,0},\ket{\rla,\rla},\ket{\rla,\gla},
\ket{\gla,\rla},\ket{\gla,\gla}}\,.

(1.3.51)

We can then define the corresponding versions of the truncated gauge fields.
As for the quadratic Casimir operator in
Eq.

1.1.55
, we have:

\hat{E}^{2}=\frac{3}{4}\rm{diag}(0|+1,+1,+1,+1)\,.

(1.3.52)

Correspondingly, the truncated parallel transport reduces to
[
595
]
:

\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}={\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\quantity(
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
0&{+}\delta_{\alpha\rla}\delta_{\beta\gla}
& -\delta_{\alpha\rla}\delta_{\beta\rla}
&{+}\delta_{\alpha\gla}\delta_{\beta\gla}
& -\delta_{\alpha\gla}\delta_{\beta\rla}\\
\hline-\delta_{\alpha\gla} \delta_{\beta\rla}&0&0&0&0\\
-\delta_{\alpha\gla} \delta_{\beta\gla}&0&0&0&0\\
{+}\delta_{\alpha\rla} \delta_{\beta\rla}&0&0&0&0\\
{+}\delta_{\alpha\rla} \delta_{\beta\gla}&0&0&0&0\\
\end{array})\,,

(1.3.53)

where the
1/\sqrt{2}
factor ensures that the hopping term preserves the state norm on its support.
Similarly, also the rishon-decomposition in
Eq.

1.3.23
can be simplified as follows:

\hat{U}^{\alpha\beta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}^{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\beta\dagger}\,,

(1.3.54)

where
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons in
Eq.

1.3.27
reduce to:

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}r}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\outerproduct{0}{\rla}{+}\outerproduct{\gla}{0}}{\sqrt[4]{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{2}}\quantity(
\begin{array}{c|cc}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
\hline 0 & 0 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
\end{array})_{F}

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\outerproduct{0}{\gla}{-}\outerproduct{\rla}{0}}{\sqrt[4]{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{2}}\quantity(
\begin{array}{c|cc}
0 & 0 & 1 \\
\hline{-}1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 \\
\end{array})_{F}\,,

(1.3.55)

with the corresponding parity operator

\hat{P}_{\zeta}=+\outerproduct{0}{0}-(\outerproduct{\rla}{\rla}+\outerproduct{\gla}{\gla})=\rm{diag}(+1|-1,-1)

(1.3.56)

and SU(2) rishon-generators

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{1/2}^{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\begin{array}{c|cc}
0& \\
\hline& 0&1\\
& 1&0\\
\end{array})

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{1/2}^{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\begin{array}{c|cc}
0& \\
\hline& 0& -i\\
& i&0\\
\end{array})

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{1/2}^{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\begin{array}{c|cc}
0& \\
\hline& 1&0\\
& 0& -1\\
\end{array})\,.

By definition, the spin-Hilbert space of every side of the link
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
is 3-dimensional:

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}=\quantity{\ket{0},\ket{\rla},\ket{\gla}}=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}.

(1.3.66)

Correspondingly, the Hilbert space of the whole link
\mathcal{H}_{\text{link}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}
has 9 states.
To recover the original 5-dimensional space in
Eq.

1.3.51
, we must constrain the left and right rishons on each link to be in the same spin shell
j
by selecting the even parity sector of a local
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry defined in
Eq.

1.3.36
.

(1+1)D hardcore-gluon SU(2) LGT: a qudit model

In one spatial dimension, the Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.3.7
reduces to

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\rm{SU(2)}}^{\rm{\rm{1D}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\quantity[-i\hpsi^{\dagger}_{\vecsite, \alpha} \NApara_{\genlink}\hpsi_{\siteplus,\beta}+\hc]+m_{0}c^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}(-1)^{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}+\frac{g^{2}c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}\hat{E}^{2}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}.

(1.3.67)

Correspondingly, the dressed-site formalism obtained in the
hardcore-gluon
approximation results in a 6-dimensional local basis
[
578
]
:

\quantity{
\ket{\alpha} =\ket{\mR^{\alpha}({\vecsite-\latvec,\vecsite}),\charge^{\alpha}(\vecsite),\mL^{\alpha}({\genlink})}
}_{\alpha=1}^{6}

(1.3.68)

which is made of the following states

\displaystyle\ket{1}

\displaystyle=\ket{0,0,0},

\displaystyle\ket{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ket{\rla,0,\gla}-\ket{\gla,0,\rla}}{\sqrt{2}},

(1.3.69)

\displaystyle\ket{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ket{\gla,\rla,0}-\ket{\rla,\gla,0}}{\sqrt{2}},

\displaystyle\ket{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ket{0,\rla,\gla}-\ket{0,\gla,\rla}}{\sqrt{2}},

\displaystyle\ket{5}

\displaystyle=\ket{0,2,0},

\displaystyle\ket{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ket{\rla,2,\gla}-\ket{\gla,2,\rla}}{\sqrt{2}}.

Similarly to the procedure in
Section

1.3.4
, the construction of the dressed-site operators and their expression in the 6-dimensional qudit basis in
Eq.

1.3.69
follows:

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{\mathbf{n},L}=\hat{Q}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\outerproduct{1}{4}+\outerproduct{2}{3}+\outerproduct{3}{6}+\sqrt{2}\outerproduct{4}{5},

(1.3.70a)

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{\mathbf{n},R}=\hat{Q}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}}=-i\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\zeta}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\outerproduct{1}{3}+\outerproduct{2}{4}+\sqrt{2}\outerproduct{3}{5}+\outerproduct{4}{6},

(1.3.70b)

\displaystyle\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n}}=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{3}{3}+\outerproduct{4}{4}+2\outerproduct{5}{5}+2\outerproduct{6}{6},

(1.3.70c)

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\casimir_{\vecsite, +\latvec}+\casimir_{\vecsite, -\latvec}]

\displaystyle=2\outerproduct{2}{2}+\outerproduct{3}{3}+\outerproduct{4}{4}+2\outerproduct{6}{6}\,.

(1.3.70d)

For potential quantum simulation implementations, it is convenient to rewrite
Eq.

1.3.67
solely in terms of Hermitian operators, introducing
[
578
]

\displaystyle\hat{A}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\hat{Q}_{L}+\hat{Q}_{L}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\hat{Q}_{R}+\hat{Q}_{R}^{\dagger},

(1.3.71)

\displaystyle\hat{A}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=i\quantity[\hat{Q}_{L}-\hat{Q}_{L}^{\dagger}],

\displaystyle\hat{B}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=i\quantity[\hat{Q}_{R} - \hat{Q}_{R}^{\dagger}].

Noting that
(\hat{A}^{(1)}\otimes\hat{A}^{(1)}+\hat{A}^{(2)}\otimes\hat{A}^{(2)})=2(\hat{Q}_{L}\otimes\hat{Q}_{R}^{\dagger}+\hat{Q}_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes\hat{Q}_{R})

Finally, we map
Eq.

1.3.67
to the following 6-dimensional qudit Hamiltonian
[
438
]
:

\hat{H}_{\rm{SU(2)}}^{\rm{\rm{1D}}}=\frac{c\hbar}{4a_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\sum_{k\in\{1,2\}}\quantity[\hat{A}^{(k)}_{\vecsite}\hat{B}^{(k)}_{\vecsite+\latvec}]+m_{0}c^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}(-1)^{\mathbf{n}}\hat{M}_{\mathbf{n}}+\frac{g^{2}c\hbar}{4a_{0}}\sum_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}\,.

(1.3.72)

Since the basis in
Eq.

1.3.69
is already gauge invariant, the Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.3.72
is tailored for TN simulations as well as to a six-level trapped-ion qudit quantum processor
[
601
]
.
In
[
578
]
, the authors discuss the experimental feasibility of using generalized Mølmer-Sørensen gates to efficiently simulate dynamics of
Eq.

1.3.72
.
A shallow circuit with these resources is demonstrated to be sufficient for implementing scalable digital quantum simulations of the model.
Numerical simulations of the corresponding dynamics manifest physically-relevant properties specific to non-Abelian field theories, such as baryon excitations
[
578
]
, and compelling non-ergodic behaviors compatible with quantum many-body scars
[
438
]
and detailed in
Section

4.4
.

(2+1)D hardcore-gluon SU(2) LGT

In two spatial dimensions, where the logical site is structured as in
Section

1.3.4
, the previous 6-dimensional dressed-site basis becomes a 30-dimensional Hilbert space.
The first 9 states are combinations of only gauge fields:

\displaystyle\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\ket{\scriptstyle{\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{0.8}\begin{array}{@{\hspace{0.3ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.3ex}}}
& {0} & \\
{0} & {0} & {0} \\
& {0} &
\end{array}}}

(1.3.73)

\displaystyle\ket{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{\scriptstyle{\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{0.8}\begin{array}{@{\hspace{0.3ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.3ex}}}
& {\gla} & \\
{0} & {0} & {\rla} \\
& {0} &
\end{array}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{\scriptstyle{\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{0.8}\begin{array}{@{\hspace{0.3ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.3ex}}}
& {\rla} & \\
{0} & {0} & {\gla} \\
& {0} &
\end{array}}}\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\ket{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{\scriptstyle{\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{0.8}\begin{array}{@{\hspace{0.3ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.7ex}}c@{\hspace{0.3ex}}}
& {\gla} & \\
{0} & {0} & {0} \\
& {\rla} &

,

and

[

]
and detailed in
Chapter

3
.

1.4
U(1) Lattice Gauge Theory

In this section, we apply the dressed-site formalism developed in
Section

1.2
to an Abelian scenario, focusing on the U(1) LGT including dynamical matter, i.e. the lattice realization of quantum-electrodynamics (QED).
Again, for simplicity we will consider a (2+1)D system, where all the gauge (electric and magnetic) contributions play a non-trivial role.

1.4.1
The model

Within the Wilson and Kogut-Susskind formulation of LGTs
[
449
,
579
]
, the U(1) version of the general Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.1.65
reads:

\begin{split}H_{\rm{U(1)}}=&\frac{c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\quantity[-i\hpsi_{\vecsite}^{\dagger}\Apara_{\vecsite, \latvec[x]}\hpsi_{\vecsite+\latvec[x]}
- (-1)^{\site[x]+\site[y]}\hpsi_{\vecsite}\Apara_{\vecsite,\latvec[y]}\hpsi_{\vecsite+\latvec[y]}+\hc]\\
&+m_{0}c^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}(-1)^{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}+\frac{g^{2}c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}\hat{E}^{2}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}-\frac{c\hbar}{2g^{2}a_{0}}\sum_{\square}\Tr(\plaq+ \plaq*)\,,\end{split}

(1.4.1)

where, since there is only one generator, the electric field is unique
\hat{L}=\hat{R}=\hat{E}
and the parallel transporter acts as a raising operator:

\displaystyle\quantity[\Apara,\eleE]

\displaystyle=\hat{U}

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{\dagger}\hat{U}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}

\displaystyle\quantity[\Apara, \Apara^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=0\,.

(1.4.2)

In the electric basis representation, where
\hat{E}
is diagonal,
Eq.

1.4.2
can be obtained assuming the following behaviors: given the
\ket{\ell}
electric state of the gauge link Hilbert space,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\hat{E}\ket{\ell}=\ell\ket{\ell}\\
\hat{U}\ket{\ell}=\ket{\ell-1}\\
\hat{U}^{\dagger}\ket{\ell}=\ket{\ell+1}\\
\end{cases}

so that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\hat{E}\hat{U}\ket{\ell}=(\ell-1)\ket{\ell-1}\\
\hat{U}\hat{E}\ket{\ell}=\ell\ket{\ell-1}\\
\end{cases}\,.

(1.4.3)

Correspondingly, U(1) Gauss law requires that
G_{\mathbf{n}}\ket{\Psi_{\rm{phys}}}=0

\forall\mathbf{n}
, where
G_{\mathbf{n}}
is the local generator of the U(1) gauge symmetry, satisfies
\commutator*{G_{\vecsite}}{\ham}=0
, and is defined as follows:

\hat{G}_{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{E}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}-\hat{E}_{{\mathbf{n}-{\bf\it\mu},{\bf\it\mu}}}-\psi_{\mathbf{n}}^{\dagger}\psi_{\mathbf{n}}+\frac{1-(-1)^{\mathbf{n}}}{2}\,.

(1.4.4)

1.4.2
Truncating the U(1) gauge group

To perform numerical simulations of
Eq.

1.4.1
, we need to perform a truncation of the infinite algebra of the U(1) gauge fields.
As discussed in
Section

1.2.1
, one possible solution is to express the gauge operators
\hat{E}
and
\hat{U}
directly in terms of spin operators in the
j
representation of SU(2):

\displaystyle\hat{E}

\displaystyle=\hat{S}^{z}(j)

\displaystyle\hat{U}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j}\hat{S}^{-}(j)\,.

(1.4.5)

Correspondingly, the gauge link Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\rm{link}}
is made out of
(2j+1)
states
\ket{\ell}
, which can be labeled as
\ket{\spin,m}
, where
-j\leq m\geq j
is the third component of the spin momentum
j
.
According to the rules of angular momentum summation, we would have:

\begin{cases}\hat{E}\ket{\spin,m}=S^{z}\ket{\spin,m}=m\ket{\spin,m}\\
\hat{U}\ket{\spin,m}=\frac{S^{-}}{j}\ket{\spin,m}=\sqrt{(1-\frac{1}{j})-\frac{m}{j^{2}}(m-1)}\ket{\spin,m-1}\\
\hat{U}^{\dagger}\ket{\spin,m}=\frac{S^{+}}{j}\ket{\spin,m}=\sqrt{(1+\frac{1}{j})-\frac{m}{j^{2}}(m+1)}\ket{\spin,m+1}\,,\end{cases}

(1.4.6)

which yields to the following commutation relations:

\begin{split}\quantity[\Apara,\eleE]&=\frac{1}{j}\quantity[\hat{S}^{-}(\spin),\hat{S}^{z}(\spin)]=\frac{\hat{S}^{-}(j)}{j}=\hat{U}\\
\quantity[\Apara,\Apara^{\dagger}]&=\frac{1}{j^{2}}\quantity[\hat{S}^{-}(\spin),\hat{S}^{+}(\spin)]=-\frac{2\hat{S}^{z}(j)}{j^{2}}\underset{j\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.\end{split}

(1.4.7)

Such a definition of parallel transport provides a uniform convergence to
Eq.

1.4.2
in the large-
j
limit.
Similarly, we can define
\hat{U}=\rm{diag}_{-1}(+1,\dots,+1)
as a ladder operator, which is perfectly unitary in the core, yet badly converges in the (upper and lower) truncated states.
In the limit of
j\to\infty
, both solutions recover
Eq.

1.4.3
.
As we will discuss in
Section

1.4.5
, the error induced by the truncation is particularly relevant in the small coupling limit, while remaining negligible in most scenarios
[
547
,
481
]
.
Choosing the ladder operator can reveal optimal for qubit base quantum hardware
[
602
]
, while
Eq.

1.4.5
is preferable in case of quantum systems that allow for the implementation of interacting spin chains with large spins
[
603
]
.
For completeness, we mention that there are mappings of the parallel transporter preserving unitarity
[
604
,
605
,
606
,
607
]
.

1.4.3
Rishon decomposition of U(1) gauge fields

The next step towards the dressed-site formalism is decomposing the
(2j+1)
states of the link basis as a combination of two states, one per half-link:
\ket{\spin,m}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\ket{\spin,m_{\vecsite,+\latvec},m_{\siteplus,-\latvec}}
.
Omitting the
j
-label, the truncated link Hilbert space reads:

\mathcal{H}_{j}=\quantity{\ket{+\spin,-\spin},\ket{+\spin-1,-\spin+1},\dots, \ket{m,m},\dots\ket{-\spin+1,+\spin-1},\ket{-\spin,+\spin}}\,.

(1.4.8)

In the Euclidean basis, these gauge link states would read:

\displaystyle\ket{+\spin,-\spin}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\ket{+\spin-1,-\spin+1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\ket{-\spin+1,+\spin-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\ket{-\spin,+\spin}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.4.9)

Then, we express the parallel transport
\hat{U}
as the product of two rishon modes
\hat{\zeta}_{L}
and
\hat{\zeta}_{R}
:

\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n}}\hat{\zeta}_{B,{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\dagger}

(1.4.10)

where, in full generality, we assumed the two rishon modes of the
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
-link to belong to two different species, A and B
3
3

3

It is possible to prove that, in
semi-integer
spin-representations, the two species coincide.
.
A general definition for the rishon operators is

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&a_{1}&&&\\
&0&a_{2}&&\\
&&0&a_{3}&\\
&&&0&\ddots\\
\end{pmatrix}_{F}

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&b_{1}&&&\\
&0&b_{2}&&\\
&&0&b_{3}&\\
&&&0&\ddots\\
\end{pmatrix}_{F}\,,

(1.4.11)

where
\quantity{a_{i}}_{i=1\dots 2s}
and
\quantity{b_{i}}_{i=1\dots 2s}
are complex numbers to be determined in the specific spin-
s
representation of U(1).
To obtain an operative Hamiltonian that is fully bosonic in every term, we require the rishon modes to satisfy a fermionic algebra.
Namely,
\hat{\zeta}
-rishons must anti-commute among themselves and with matter fields:

\displaystyle\quantity{\rishon_{A,\genlink},\rishon_{B,\siteplus,-\latvec}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\quantity{\rishon_{\genlink},\hpsi_{\vecsite}}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.4.12)

Being fermions,
\hat{\zeta}
-rishon must also satisfy (
1.2.7
).
We define the rishon parity operator as

P^{\rm{U(1)}}_{\hat{\zeta}}=\rm{diag}(+1,-1,+1,-1,\dots)\,,

(1.4.13)

where by convention we establish the first rishon state to be
even
, while the remaining ones are determined by alternating the sign of parity.
Then, the last state is even (odd) if the spin-
j
representation is
integer
(
semi-integer
).

We can then properly express the parallel transport in (
1.4.10
) as follows:

\begin{split}\hat{U}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=&\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}\hat{\zeta}_{B,{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}^{\dagger}=\quantity[\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec} \otimes\mathbb{1}_{\siteplus, -\latvec}]\times\quantity[\parity_{\zeta,\vecsite, +\latvec} \otimes\;\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\siteplus, -\latvec}]\\
=&\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}\cdot\hat{P}_{\zeta,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{B,{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}},-{\bf\it\mu}}\\
=&\underset{\mathbf{n}}{\begin{pmatrix}0&-a_{1}&&&\\
&0&+a_{2}&&\\
&&0&-a_{3}&\\
&&&&\ddots\\
\end{pmatrix}}\otimes\underset{{\mathbf{n}+{\bf\it\mu}}}{\begin{pmatrix}0&&&&\\
\overline{b}_{1}&0&&&\\
&\overline{b}_{2}&0&&\\
&&\overline{b}_{3}&0&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
\end{pmatrix}}.\end{split}

(1.4.14)

The correct matrix elements
\quantity{a_{i}}_{i=1\dots 2j_{\max}}
and
\quantity{b_{i}}_{i=1\dots 2j_{\max}}
of the two
\hat{\zeta}
-rishon modes in the specific spin-
j_{\max}
representation for
U(1)
are the ones matching the original choice of the parallel transporter (spin operator as in
Eq.

1.4.5
or ladder operator).

Example: spin-1 representation of U(1)

As an example, we derive the expression of the gauge fields (in terms of rishons) for the
j=1
-spin representation.
Such a truncation is the smallest non-trivial
integer
representation that gives access to non trivial features of the U(1) LGT
[
608
,
525
,
522
]
.

Within this truncation, the gauge fields read:

\displaystyle\hat{E}=\hat{S}^{z}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}+1&&\\
&0&\\
&&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\hat{U}=\hat{S}^{-}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&&\\
1&0&\\
&1&0&\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1.4.15)

The corresponding link Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{j=1}
defined in (
1.4.9
) is made out of the following states:

\displaystyle\ket{1,1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\ket{0,0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\ket{-1,-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1.4.16)

Then, from the definition of
\hat{U}
in (
1.4.15
), the only relevant matrix elements of
\hat{\zeta}_{A}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{B}
are the ones that correspond to the action on sites belonging to (
1.4.9
):

\begin{aligned} \hat{\zeta}_{A}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{B}\ket{1,1}&=+a_{2}\overline{b}_{1}\ket{0,0}\\
\hat{\zeta}_{A}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{B}\ket{0,0}&=-a_{1}\overline{b}_{2}\ket{-1,-1}\\
\hat{\zeta}_{A}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{B}\ket{-1,-1}&=0\end{aligned}\qquad\text{whose solution could be}\qquad\begin{aligned} a_{1}&=a_{2}=1\\
b_{1}&=+\sqrt{2}=-b_{2}.\end{aligned}

(1.4.17)

Summarizing, we have found

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&+1&\\
&0&+1\\
&&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&+\sqrt{2}&\\
&0&-\sqrt{2}\\
&&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1.4.18)

Notice that, by using the ladder definition for the parallel transporter, the two rishons read:

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&+1&\\
&0&+1\\
&&0\\
\end{pmatrix}_{F}

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&+1&\\
&0&-1\\
&&0\\
\end{pmatrix}{F}\,.

(1.4.19)

1.4.4
Constructing the dressed-site operators

We stress that the rishon decomposition is a general approach and can be applied to lattices of whatever dimension
D
.
However, as discussed for SU(2), for a fixed
D
, the construction of the Hamiltonian operators requires attention in choosing an internal ordering of the dressed-site basis.
Working in a
D=2
-dimensional lattice, the single dressed-site basis can be sketched as:

\ket{\begin{array}{ccc}
&\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[y]}&\\
\rishon_{B,\vecsite,-\latvec[x]}&\psi_{\vecsite}&\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\\
&\rishon_{B,\vecsite,-\latvec[y]}&\\
\end{array}}\quad\text{where the d.o.f. are ordered as:}\quad\ket{\begin{array}{ccc}
&4&\\
1&0&3\\
&2&\\
\end{array}}\,.

(1.4.20)

Every dressed-site operator is then constructed as in (
1.2.6
) according to the previous order.
We are then ready to express each Hamiltonian term in terms of dressed-site operators.

Hopping operators

In the hopping Hamiltonian, the matter-gauge interaction (apart from the staggered factors) along the link
({\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}})
can be rewritten in terms of two bosonic
arrival
operators.
Namely

\begin{split}\hat{H}^{\text{hopping}}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}&=\quantity[\hpsi^{\dagger}_{\vecsite}\hat{U}_{\genlink}\hpsi_{\vecsite+\latvec}+\hc]\\
&=\quantity[\hpsi^{\dagger}_{\vecsite}\rishon_{A,\vecsite+\latvec}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec,-\latvec}\hpsi_{\vecsite+\latvec}+\hc]\\
&=\quantity[\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{\vecsite,+\latvec}\hat{Q}_{\vecsite+\latvec,-\latvec}+\hc]\end{split}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\begin{aligned} \hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}=\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}\\
\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}}=\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}}.\end{aligned}

(1.4.21)

Then, according to the internal ordering of dressed site defined in (
1.4.20
), we have for instance:

\begin{split}\hat{Q}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}=\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}=&\quad\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\\
&\times\hat{P}_{\psi,\mathbf{n}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\\
=&\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\hat{P}_{\psi,\mathbf{n}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\,.\end{split}

(1.4.22)

Number density operators

Similarly, number density operators can be expressed as:

\begin{split}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n}}&=\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\psi_{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}\bigotimes_{\pm{\bf\it\mu}}\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}\,.\end{split}

(1.4.23)

Magnetic operators

Let us rewrite the single plaquette interaction in terms of rishon modes.
The idea is to perform a series of fermionic swaps to make the rishons of the same dressed site close in the internal ordering of the plaquette.
We have then:

\begin{split}\hat{U}_{\mathbf{\square}}^{\vphantom{dagger}}=&\quantity[\Apara_{\genlink_{x}}\Apara_{\vecsite+\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]}
\Apara^{\dagger}_{\vecsite+\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]}\Apara^{\dagger}_{\genlink_{y}}]\\
=&\Big[\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x],-\latvec[x]})\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]})\times\\
&\qquad\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[x]})^{\dagger}\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[y]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]})^{\dagger}\Big]\\
=&\Big[\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x],-\latvec[x]})\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]})\times\\
&\qquad\quantity(\rishon_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[x]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]})\quantity(\rishon_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[y]})\Big]\\
=&\Big[\quantity(\rishon_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}\rishon^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite,+\latvec[y]})\quantity(\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x],-\latvec[x]}\rishon_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]})\times\\
&\qquad\quantity(\rishon^{\dagger}_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]}\rishon_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y],-\latvec[x]})\quantity(\rishon^{\dagger}_{A,\vecsite+\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]}\rishon_{B,\vecsite+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y]})\Big]\\
=&\quantity[-\corner_{\vecsite, +\latvec[x],+\latvec[y]}\corner_{\vecsite+\latvec[x], +\latvec[y],-\latvec[x]}\corner_{\vecsite+\latvec[x]+\latvec[y], -\latvec[x],-\latvec[y]}\corner_{\vecsite+\latvec[y],-\latvec[y],+\latvec[x]}]\,,\end{split}

(1.4.24)

where we merged rishon modes belonging to the same dressed site into
corner
operators defined as
\hat{C}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}=\hat{\zeta}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{1}}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}_{2}}
.
For instance, from the internal order of the dressed-site basis in (
1.4.20
), we have for instance:

\begin{split}\hat{C}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}=&\quad\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\\
=&\quad\hat{P}_{\psi,\mathbf{n}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\\
=&\times\hat{P}_{\psi,\mathbf{n}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\\
=&\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}_{B,\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\cdot\hat{P}_{\hat{\zeta},\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}\otimes\hat{\zeta}^{\dagger}_{A,\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}_{y}}.\end{split}

(1.4.25)

Electric field operators

In the dressed-site formalism, as with the parallel transporter, the electric field contribution is equally divided across the two half-links, with each half contributing half the total electric energy.
Namely, from (
1.4.5
) we have:

\hat{E}^{2}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\hat{S}^{z}_{\vecsite,\latvec}(\spin)^{2}+\hat{S}_{\siteplus,-\latvec}^{z}(\spin)^{2}].

(1.4.26)

In the operative Hamiltonian, we combine the
2D
half-links contribution to the electric energy onto the same single dressed-site operator:

\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm{\bf\it\mu}}\hat{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}(j)^{2}.

(1.4.27)

Operative dressed-site U(1) Hamiltonian

We can then rewrite the (2+1)D U(1) truncated Hamiltonian in
Eq.

1.4.1
as follows:

\begin{split}H_{\rm{U(1)}}=&\frac{c\hbar}{2a_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\quantity[\text{-i}\hat{Q}_{\vecsite,+\latvec[x]}^{\dagger}\hat{Q}_{\vecsite+\latvec[x], -\latvec[x]}
- (-1)^{\vecsite}\hat{Q}_{\vecsite,+\latvec[y]}^{\dagger}\hat{Q}_{\vecsite+\latvec[y], -\latvec[y]} + \hc]\\
&+m_{0}c^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}(-1)^{\mathbf{n}}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{n}}+\frac{g^{2}c\hbar}{4a_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{n}}-\frac{c\hbar}{2g^{2}a_{0}}\sum_{\square}\Tr(\plaq+ \plaq*)\,.\end{split}

(1.4.28)

U(1) Link-Symmetry operators

The equivalence between
Eq.

1.4.1
and
Eq.

1.4.28
is correct as long as the link symmetries are satisfied, i.e., as long as the rishon states of each link have opposite electric fields.
This constraint reduces to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry on each link that can be easily resolved with dedicated libraries
[
441
,
609
]
, or imposed as two-body penalty terms (one per link) in the Hamiltonian, in such a way to behave as the largest energy scale of the problem.

Open boundary conditions and half-integer representations

In the case of open boundary conditions (OBC), we may want to freeze the half links on the bordering dressed sites of the lattice to the ground of the electric field.
For
integer

j
-representations, this can be easily achieved by selecting the rishon configurations with
\ket{\spin, 0}
on sites along the corresponding border
{\bf\it\mu}
.
Equivalently, for each of these sites, we can add a penalty term like
\hat{H}^{\text{border}}_{{\mathbf{n},{\bf\it\mu}}}=\alpha\quantity(\eleE_{\genlink}-\spin)^{2}
.
As for
semi-integer
representations, where the minimal electric field is degenerate, we can choose between one of the following penalties:

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{\text{border}}_{\mathbf{n},-{\bf\it\mu}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\quantity(\eleE_{\vecsite,-\latvec}-\lceil\spin\rceil)^{2}

and

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{\text{border}}_{\mathbf{n},+{\bf\it\mu}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\quantity(\eleE_{\vecsite,+\latvec}-\lfloor\spin\rfloor)^{2}\,,

(1.4.29)

where
\lceil j\rceil
(
\lfloor j\rfloor
) corresponds to the minimum upper (maximum lower) integer representation.

Projecting operators on the U(1) gauge-invariant dressed-site basis

As discussed in
Section

1.2.3
, all the previous operators need to be projected in the U(1) gauge-invariant subspace.
Since we have chosen the staggered fermions formulation, we expect two gauge invariant bases, one for
even
and one for
odd
sites, respectively
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
.
Each one coincides with the kernel of the corresponding U(1) Gauss Law operator
\hat{G}_{\mathbf{n},p}
:

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{\mathbf{n},p}M_{p}=\quantity[\hat{N}_{\vecsite}+\sum_{\latvec}\hat{E}_{\latvec}-\frac{1-p}{2}\mathbb{1}_{\vecsite}]M_{p}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.4.30)

where
p=\pm 1
is the parity of the lattice site.

1.4.5
Scaling of the local basis dimension

Of course, the larger the gauge field truncation (i.e. the larger the spin-
j
representation), the larger the effective dressed-site Hilbert space.
In
Table

1.1
, we list the dressed-site dimension
d=\dim\mathcal{H}_{\text{dress}}
associated with the first few nontrivial gauge truncations of U(1) and SU(2), detailed in
Section

1.3
in
D=2,3
space dimensions.
Both the considered LGTs include dynamical matter, represented by one fermionic field multiplet in the fundamental representation of the gauge group.
The
\ell
-th row of
Table

1.1
is obtained keeping only the first
\ell
nonzero electric energy levels (i.e., using the
\ell
-th smallest quadratic Casimir eigenvalue as cutoff
\Theta
, see
Section

1.2.1
).
As
Table

1.1
shows, the local dimension increases rapidly with
\ell
.
For (3+1)D non-Abelian LGTs,
d\sim O(10^{4})
is reached already within the first two truncations, making the study of the untruncated limit prohibitive.

As it will be discussed in
Section

2.6
, differently from the models typically encountered in condensed matter physics, the local dimension of LGTs can thus be a limiting factor for Tensor Network simulations | especially when
d
becomes comparable to commonly used TN bond dimensions (
100\lesssim\chi\lesssim 500
for TTN).
In these cases, crucially for high-dimensional LGTs, strategies aimed at further compressing the local computational basis are needed (see
Section

2.6.1
).

for increasing number

of allowed electric energy density levels in some (2+1)D and (3+1)D paradigmatic LGTs with dynamical matter and gauge groups U(1) and SU(2).

[

.

:
Exact diagonalization of a QED plaquette for a grid of masses
m\in[10^{-2},10^{1}]
and couplings
g^{2}\in[10^{-1},10^{1}]
.
(a,b) Minimal gauge truncation
\ell^{*}
required to reach a precision
\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}=10^{-5}
in the magnetic energy
\expectationvalue*{\Re\plaq}
.
(c) Corresponding entanglement entropy
S(\ell^{*})
associated with a symmetric bipartition of the plaquette.
Figure from
[

]
.

Several numerical analyses suggest that, in some cases, a small-to-moderate truncation of the gauge group is enough for accurately approximating the continuum limits, at least for low-energy states
[
516
,
517
,
522
,
610
,
611
,
612
]
.
However, the optimal gauge truncation depends on the Hamiltonian parameters
m_{0}
and
g
.

In order understand this behavior, we focus on a single QED plaquette in open boundary conditions, as it provides the minimal setting allowing for both electric and magnetic effects.
Then, to characterize the convergence in the gauge truncation
\ell
, we consider a candidate observable
O
and compute its ground state expectation value
\langle\hat{O}\rangle_{\ell}
for increasing
\ell
.
We iterate until the relative deviation between consecutive truncations drops below some threshold
\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}
:

|\langle\hat{O}\rangle_{\ell^{*}}-\langle\hat{O}\rangle_{\ell^{*}-1}|<\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}|\langle\hat{O}\rangle_{\ell^{*}}|
for some
\ell^{*}
.

Fig.

1.3
(a) shows the minimal truncation
\ell^{*}
at which the magnetic energy operator
\hat{O}=\real\hat{U}_{\mathbf{\square}}^{\vphantom{dagger}}
is converged to
\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}=10^{-5}
.
We explore a grid of model parameters, whose extent has been chosen according to standard MC literature
[
613
,
614
,
615
,
470
,
464
,
465
,
466
,
471
,
444
,
616
,
617
,
618
,
581
]
.

\real\hat{U}_{\mathbf{\square}}^{\vphantom{dagger}}
is used as a benchmark due to its relevance in the weak coupling regime, where the continuum limit of
D<3
lattice QED is located
[
464
]
.
As
Fig.

1.3
(b) shows,
\ell^{*}
depends heavily on the coupling, growing asymptotically like
\ell^{*}\sim g^{-1}
as
g
is decreased, while
m_{0}
plays almost no role.
An analogous inverse dependence of the minimal gauge truncation on the coupling is expected for non-Abelian LGT in arbitrary dimensions.
Moreover, the continuum limit of non-Abelian LGTs in
D\leq 3
is also expected at
g\to 0

[
464
]
, further substantiating the need to compress the local dimension in TN simulations of LGT whenever extrapolation to the continuum is in order.
Developements along this directions represent an ongoing research line we are considering towards more accurate simulations.

Apart from the growth of the local dimension, extrapolation to the continuum is further complicated by the fact that the continuum limit of a lattice quantum field theory corresponds to a critical point of the underlying lattice model
[
447
]
.
Close to criticality, quantum correlations are boosted and violations of the entanglement area law are expected
[
619
]
.
The higher entanglement entropy in the proximity of the continuum (
g,m_{0}\ll 1
regime) is already captured by the single-plaquette analysis of
Fig.

1.3
(c) (see
[
437
]
for the SU(2) case).
Continuum limits of LGTs are thus an area of potential advantage for quantum computation over classical methods, as the former is not limited by entanglement.
Nonetheless, quantum computation is also affected by the need to relax gauge truncations when
g^{2}\to 0
, either by increasing the number of qubits used to encode a dressed site, which is at least
\lceil\log_{2}(d)\rceil
, or by using hybrid devices with both qubits and bosonic modes
[
620
]
.

1.5
Summary

In this chapter, we provided a detailed exploration of Hamiltonian Lattice Gauge Theories (LGTs) with an emphasis on the discretization process for both fermionic and gauge fields.
We began by reviewing the lattice discretization of fermions, highlighting the staggered fermion approach to mitigate the fermion doubling problem.
The discretization of gauge fields was then discussed, where the continuous gauge fields were replaced by parallel transporters, ensuring lattice gauge invariance.
We detailed how the discretization of the electric and magnetic components of the gauge fields, leading to the construction of the lattice Hamiltonian.
The dimensional analysis was carried out, emphasizing the role of the gauge coupling and its scaling with lattice spacing.

The second part of the chapter introduced the dressed-site formalism, which effectively integrates the gauge fields into matter sites, reducing the complexity of the gauge theory and making it more amenable to TN algorithms and quantum simulations.
This formalism has been thoroughly applied to two exemplary cases, such as the non-Abelian SU(2) Yang-Mills in
Section

1.3
and the Abelian U(1) LGTs in
Section

1.4
.
The techniques and concepts introduced here for these two models will be foundational for understanding the numerical methods and physical results presented and investigated later in the subsequent chapters of this thesis.

Chapter 2
Tensor Networks methods for LGTs

The rich phenomenology of complex quantum many-body (QMB) systems is often explored using advanced numerical techniques.
For many quantum Hamiltonians, exact solutions become computationally infeasible as the system size increases, due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space.
For a system with
N
particles and a local dimension
d
, the dimensionality of the Hilbert space grows as
\dim,\mathcal{H}=d^{N}
, leading to a
d^{N}\times d^{N}
Hamiltonian matrix.
Exact diagonalization (ED), while valuable for small systems, quickly becomes impractical, requiring memory beyond the limits of most hardware as system size increases.

Given these challenges, alternative numerical approaches have emerged, with two key methods standing out: Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations
[
621
]
and Tensor Network (TN) methods
[
483
]
.
QMC techniques use stochastic methods to solve the Schrd̈inger equation as a diffusion problem, while TN approaches leverage the entanglement properties of quantum systems to efficiently represent low-energy states, retaining only the most relevant contributions to equilibrium properties and real-time dynamics.

Both QMC and TN methods have significantly advanced our ability to simulate QMB systems, providing insights into ground state properties, phase transitions, and dynamic behaviors across various domains.
Their applications extend beyond condensed matter physics, with relevance in fields such as nuclear physics, chemistry
[
472
]
, material science, and even machine learning
[
483
]
.
However, these methods also face significant challenges when dealing with strongly correlated fermionic systems.
For instance, QMC suffers from the notorious
sign problem

[
622
,
473
,
448
]
, which stems from the antisymmetric nature of fermionic wave functions, leading to highly oscillatory integrals that are difficult to compute.

On the other hand, TNs provide a powerful framework for bypassing the sign problem, but they require careful handling of entanglement and dimensionality issues, particularly when applied to higher-dimensional systems.
In these contexts, optimizations that preserve locality, such as the use of space-filling curves like the Hilbert curve, become crucial for maintaining efficiency in TN algorithms on high-dimensional lattices.
Such curves help reducing the computational overhead by minimizing long-range interactions when mapping 2D lattices into effective 1D structures
[
436
]
.

The need for scalability becomes even more pressing when dealing with lattice gauge theories (LGTs), where large-scale simulations are necessary to access the continuum limit and explore complex phenomena such as non-perturbative dynamics and phase transitions.
Here, TN methods, despite their successes, face challenges related to large bond dimensions and local basis truncations.
Addressing these requires both theoretical innovations, such as optimized mappings and geometries, and computational improvements, like parallelization and hybrid techniques leveraging modern high-performance computing (HPC) infrastructures.

The structure of this chapter reflects the key elements necessary for an efficient exploration of QMB systems via ED and TN methods.
In
Section

2.1
, we begin with an introduction to exact diagonalization, discussing the importance of symmetry-based techniques like block diagonalization to reduce the effective size of the Hilbert space and the associated computational load.
Next, in
Section

2.2
, we turn to TN methods, outlining the fundamental concepts and the role of entanglement in TN algorithms
[
483
]
.
Loopless TN geometries, such as Matrix Product States (MPS) and Tree Tensor Networks (TTNs), are introduced in
Section

2.2.2
as powerful tools for simulating one-dimensional and higher-dimensional finite-size systems.
A key aspect of this section is the focus on the implementation of gauge symmetries within the TN structure, enabling a further reduction in computational costs.
In
Section

2.4
, we detail the TTN variational ground-state search algorithm used for studying equilibrium properties, while
Section

2.5
outlines time evolution algorithms, such as Time-Evolved Block Decimation (TEBD) and the Time-Dependent Variational Principle (TDVP).

The original contributions of this chapter are in
Sections

2.3
and
2.6
.
In the former, we analyze the effectiveness of different TN geometries in simulating high-dimensional systems, with a particular focus on 2D lattice models.
Here, the importance of locality-preserving mappings like the Hilbert curve is highlighted, demonstrating their role in improving the accuracy and efficiency of TN simulations
[
436
]
.
In the latter, we provide a roadmap for future developments, discussing both numerical and theoretical improvements that will enable TN methods to scale up to large LGT simulations, potentially reaching sizes and accuracies comparable to current Monte Carlo methods
[
440
]
.
In conclusion, the chapter outlines a cohesive strategy for advancing the simulation of QMB systems, combining ED, efficient TN geometries, and a vision for overcoming the remaining computational barriers in large-scale LGT simulations.

2.1
Exact Representation of Quantum Many-Body Systems

Consider a QMB system defined on a D-dimensional lattice with
N=\prod_{k}^{D}L_{k}
sites.
A generic pure QMB state
\ket{\psiqmb}
of the system lives in the tensor product of
N
local Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, each one being of finite dimension
d_{j}
1
1

1

For the moment, we are still considering the general case where system sites can have in principle different local dimension and different local Hilbert space.
Later on, we will restrict to the case of
d_{j}=d

\forall j\in\quantity{1,\dots, \Nsites}
.
:
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N}
.
The state
\ket{\psiqmb}
can be exactly expanded in terms of a complete basis set of
\mathcal{H}
:

\ket{\psiqmb}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}\ket{i_{1},\dots, i_{\Nsites}}\,,

(2.1.1)

where
\ket{i_{1},\dots,i_{\Nsites}}=\ket{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\ket{i_{\Nsites}}
represents the tensor product of the local basis vectors, while
\quantity{i_{s}}
is the canonical basis for the
s^{\text{th}}
subsystem.
As the state is pure, it is uniquely determined by the coefficient tensor
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}
, whose entries are in general complex scalars and their number scales as
d^{N}
, i.e., exponentially with the system size
N
.

In these terms, providing an exact description of the state entails knowing all the
d^{N}
complex coefficients.
This is a fundamental limitation when solving a QMB problem on a classical computer, since an exponential scaling with the number of degrees of freedom implies that the exact representation described in
Eq.

2.1.1
is computationally unfeasible for intermediate-large values of
N
, especially when the local basis becomes large as in the case of dressed-site formulated LGTs (see
Sections

1.2
and
1.4.5
).
Nonetheless, ED simulations for small lattices are crucial for validating other methods such as Tensor Networks, and offer a concrete way to understand key aspects of quantum mechanics, especially the symmetry of many-body states
[
623
]
.

2.1.1
Block Diagonalization

In the special case where the QMB system has a set of symmetries (as in LGTs), the computational cost of ED algorithms can be significantly reduced (while still remaining exponential in the system size) by using
block diagonalization
, allowing for a more efficient numerical treatment of the problem.
Indeed, when a symmetry group
G
is present in the system, the total Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed into a direct sum of smaller subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}
labeled by an irreducible representation (or quantum number)
\alpha
.
Correspondingly, the QMB Hamiltonian, which is invariant under these symmetry transformations, can be written as follows:

\hat{H}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\hat{H}_{\alpha},

(2.1.2)

where each
\hat{H}_{\alpha}
is a block corresponding to a specific symmetry sector
\alpha
.
Instead of diagonalizing the full Hamiltonian in the entire Hilbert space, one can diagonalize each block
\hat{H}_{\alpha}
independently.
This drastically reduces the computational cost, as the size of each block is much smaller than the full Hilbert space.

In the following, we review two main cases of symmetries: U(1) invariance, related to particle-number conservation, and translational invariance, related to momentum convervation.
Both symmetries are currently employed in the ED-LGT Python library
[
441
]
and exploitable for simulating any QMB system or LGT in the dressed-site formalism.

Figure 2.1
:
Illustration of block diagonalization. In absence of symmetries, we have to diagonalize the whole Hamiltonian, which explores the whole Hilbet space
\sim d^{N}
.
If the Hamiltonian has one symmetry, by constructing states labeled by the corresponding conserved quantum number, the Hamiltonian matrix breaks up into blocks that can be diagonalized independently of each other (middle).
Applying another symmetry, the blocks can be further broken up into smaller blocks (right) labeled by two different quantum numbers, etc
[
623
]
.

Particle-number conservation

The presence of a global U(1) symmetry (not local as in LGTs) is associated with the conservation of a globally conserved quantity, such as the total number of particles, charge, or spin (along a certain direction) in a QMB system.
Formally, this means that the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
commutes with a total operator
\hat{A}
:

\displaystyle\commutator{\ham}{\hat{A}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\rm{where}

\displaystyle\hat{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}

(2.1.3)

and
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}
is the particle number operator on lattice site
\mathbf{n}
.
This implies that
\hat{A}
is conserved under the time evolution generated by the Hamiltonian.
For instance, in the case of the Ising model without transverse field, the conserved quantity
\hat{A}
is the magnetization along
z
and
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{\sigma}^{z}_{\mathbf{n}}
.
In bosonic or ferminic systems like in Hubbard Hamiltonians (see
Section

5.2
), the conserved quantity is the total particle number and
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{n}_{\mathbf{n}}
is the operator that counts the number of particles at site
\mathbf{n}
.
For fermions,
\hat{n}_{\mathbf{n}}
can only take values 0 or 1 (due to the Pauli exclusion principle), while for bosons
\hat{n}_{\mathbf{n}}
can take any non-negative integer value.
As for LGTs such as the ones described in
Chapter

1
and simulated in
Chapters

3
and
4
, there is a global U(1) symmetry related to the invariants of the matter fields under rotations
\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}\to e^{i\theta}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}
and corresponds to the conservation of charge: baryon number for non-Abelian LGTs such as SU(2) Yang Mills in
Section

1.3
and electric charge for Abelian ones such as QED in
Section

1.4
.
In the former case,
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}
corresponds to the baryon number density
b
defined in
Eq.

3.1.8
; in the latter case,
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{q}_{\mathbf{n}}\equiv\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\psi}_{\mathbf{n}}-((1)^{\mathbf{n}}-1)/2

[
525
,
524
,
522
]
.
Due to the aforementioned sign-problem, accessing sectors with finite baryon number density
b
or finite electric charge is challenging for MC based algorithms, while being straightforward for ED simulations (by exploiting block diagonalization)
[
441
]
or TN methods, where the U(1) sector is directly selected in the TN ansatz, ensuring that the symmetry is preserved throughout the simulation
[
624
,
625
,
482
,
484
]
.

Momentum conservation

In a QMB system with translational symmetry, as in the case of periodic boundary conditions (PBC), the corresponding Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
is invariant under translations of particle positions or lattice sites.
This means shifting all particles or lattice sites by a unit (or a set of units) leaves the physical properties of the system unchanged
[
623
]
.
For simplicity, let us focus on a 1D lattice, whose sites are labeled by
n\in\quantity{0,\dots,\Nsites}
.

We define a translation operator
\hat{T}
which moves every particle from site
n
to site
n+1
, i.e.,
\hat{T}\ket{\site}=\ket{\site+1}
, with
\hat{T}^{N}=\mathbb{1}
.
Due to translational symmetry,
\hat{T}\hat{H}=\hat{H}\hat{T}
, meaning that
\hat{H}
and
\hat{T}
admit a common set of eigenstates.
Thanks to Bloch’s theorem, these eigenstates can be expressed as wave functions with a definite momentum, characterized by a wave vector
p=2\pi n/N
:

\ket{\varphi_{\mom}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\varphi}}}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{\varphi}-1}e^{-ipn}\hat{T}^{n}\ket{\varphi},

(2.1.4)

where
\ket{\varphi}
is a reference state.
To construct the momentum basis for given
p
, we need a set of reference states resulting in a complete set of normalizable orthogonal states. Clearly, for two states
\ket{\varphi_{\mom}}
and
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{\mom}}
to be orthogonal, the corresponding representatives must obey
\hat{T}^{r}\ket{\varphi}\neq\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\,\forall r\in\quantity{1,\dots, \Nsites}
.
Therefore, for each set of translated states
\{\ket{\varphi(r)}=\hat{T}^{r}\ket{\varphi}\}
, only one of them should be used as a reference state
[
623
]
.
The normalization factor
N_{\varphi}
in
Eq.

2.1.4
takes into account the periodicity of the reference state
\ket{\varphi}
, which in general could be smaller to
N
:
\hat{T}^{N_{\varphi}}\ket{\varphi}=\ket{\varphi}
for some
N_{\varphi}<N
.

Once we have selected an orthonormal set of reference states, we can build the corresponding momentum basis via
Eq.

2.1.4
and express the Hamiltonian as follows:

\hat{H}_{pp^{\prime}}=\matrixelement{\psi_{\mom}}{\ham}{\psi_{\mom^{\prime}}},

(2.1.5)

where, due to the translational invariance,
\hat{H}_{pp^{\prime}}=0
for
p\neq p^{\prime}
.
Eigenvalues and eigenstates of the Hamiltonian in a particular momentum sector
p
are obtained diagonalizing the block
\hat{H}_{pp}
.

When dealing with QMB systems where translational invariance involves a logical basis of
N^{b}>1
sites, the translational symmetry unit is expanded from a single site to
N^{b}
sites.
Correspondingly, we have to update the momentum vetor definition in
Eq.

2.1.4
with a normalization factor that matches the total number of unit cells
L=\frac{N}{N^{b}}
or a smaller periodicity
L_{\varphi}
:

\ket{\varphi_{\mom}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{\varphi}}}\sum_{n=0}^{L_{\varphi}-1}e^{-ipn}\hat{T}^{N^{b}n}\ket{\varphi}.

(2.1.6)

For instance, in LGTs employing the staggered fermion solution, the corresponding Hamiltonian is invariant under translations of two sites along each spatial direction.
For instance, ED simulations of the (1+1)D SU(2) Yang-Mills LGT in PBC (see
Section

4.4
and
[
438
]
) have been obtained in the zero momentum, since the scarred initial states of the dyanmics belong to that sector.

2.1.2
Basic Tensor Operations

Aside by block diagonalization and the use of symmetries, by accurately manipulating the coefficient tensor
T
in
Eq.

2.1.1
, we can further extract a lot of information, such as quantum correlations, entanglement, and few-body observables, sometimes accessing only a portion of all its entries.
The main operations which can be performed over
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}
involve the indices labelling its links.
In particular, it is possible to distinguish among at least three different kinds of operations affecting links: manipulations, contractions, and decompositions. Below, we briefly discuss each of them.
For an equivalent pictorial representation, see
Fig.

2.2
.

Manipulations

Link manipulations involve operations like permutation, which reorders tensor indices, fusion, which combines adjacent links into a single index, and splitting, which breaks down a link into multiple smaller indices.

Link permutation

It reorders the links
i_{1}\dots i_{N}
of the tensor
T
by moving the link at position
\ell
into
k=\sigma(\ell)
of the permuted tensor
T^{[p]}
:

T^{[p]}_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(N)}}=T_{i_{1}\dots i_{N}}.

(2.1.7)
